against snakes, <a href="/wiki/Sin" title="Sin">sins</a><sup id="cite_ref-saint_index_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-saint_index-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Saint Patrick</b> (<a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Patricius</i>; <a href="/wiki/Irish_language" title="Irish language">Irish</a>: <i lang="ga">Pádraig</i> <small></small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="ga-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Irish" title="Help:IPA/Irish">[ˈpˠɑːɾˠɪɟ]</a></span> or <small></small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="ga-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Irish" title="Help:IPA/Irish">[ˈpˠaːd̪ˠɾˠəɟ]</a></span>; <a href="/wiki/Welsh_language" title="Welsh language">Welsh</a>: <i lang="cy">Padrig</i>) was a fifth-century <a href="/wiki/Romano-British_culture" title="Romano-British culture">Romano-British</a> Christian <a href="/wiki/Missionary" title="Missionary">missionary</a> and <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Armagh" title="Archbishop of Armagh">bishop</a> in <a href="/wiki/Gaelic_Ireland" title="Gaelic Ireland">Ireland</a>. Known as the "Apostle of Ireland", he is the primary <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of <a href="/wiki/Ireland" title="Ireland">Ireland</a>, the other patron saints being <a href="/wiki/Brigid_of_Kildare" title="Brigid of Kildare">Brigid of Kildare</a> and <a href="/wiki/Columba" title="Columba">Columba</a>. Patrick was never formally <a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">canonised</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> having lived before the current laws of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> in these matters. Nevertheless, he is venerated as a saint in the Catholic Church, the <a href="/wiki/Lutheran_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Lutheran Church">Lutheran Church</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Ireland" title="Church of Ireland">Church of Ireland</a> (part of the <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican Communion</a>), and in the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a>, where he is regarded as <a href="/wiki/Equal-to-apostles" title="Equal-to-apostles">equal-to-the-apostles</a> and Enlightener of Ireland.<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-RitschelMichallon2022_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RitschelMichallon2022-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The dates of Patrick's life cannot be fixed with certainty, but there is general agreement that he was active as a missionary in Ireland during the fifth century. A recent biography<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> on Patrick shows a late fourth-century date for the saint is not impossible.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> According to tradition dating from the early Middle Ages, Patrick was the first <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Armagh" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Armagh">bishop of Armagh</a> and <a href="/wiki/Primacy_of_Ireland" title="Primacy of Ireland">Primate of Ireland</a>, and is credited with bringing <a href="/wiki/History_of_Christianity_in_Ireland" title="History of Christianity in Ireland">Christianity to Ireland</a>, converting a <a href="/wiki/Celtic_polytheism" class="mw-redirect" title="Celtic polytheism">pagan society</a> in the process. He has been generally so regarded ever since, despite evidence of some earlier Christian presence.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>According to Patrick's autobiographical <i>Confessio</i>, when he was about sixteen, he was captured by Irish pirates from his home in Britain and taken as a <a href="/wiki/Slavery_in_medieval_Europe" title="Slavery in medieval Europe">slave</a> to Ireland. He writes that he lived there for six years as an animal <a href="/wiki/Herder" title="Herder">herder</a> before escaping and returning to his family. After becoming a cleric, he returned to spread Christianity in northern and western Ireland. In later life, he served as a bishop, but little is known about where he worked. By the seventh century, he had already come to be revered as the patron saint of Ireland.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Saint_Patrick%27s_Day" title="Saint Patrick&#39;s Day">His feast day</a> is observed on 17 March, the supposed date of his death. It is celebrated in Ireland and among the <a href="/wiki/Irish_diaspora" title="Irish diaspora">Irish diaspora</a> as a religious and cultural holiday. In the dioceses of Ireland, it is both a <a href="/wiki/Solemnity" title="Solemnity">solemnity</a> and a <a href="/wiki/Holy_day_of_obligation" title="Holy day of obligation">holy day of obligation</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Sources">Sources</span></h2>
<p>Two <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a> works survive which are generally accepted as having been written by St. Patrick. These are the <i>Declaration</i> (<a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Confessio</i>)<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> and the <i>Letter to the soldiers of Coroticus</i> (<a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Epistola</i>),<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> from which come the only generally accepted details of his life.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> The <i>Declaration</i> is the more biographical of the two. In it, Patrick gives a short account of his life and his mission. Most available details of his life are from subsequent <a href="/wiki/Hagiographies" class="mw-redirect" title="Hagiographies">hagiographies</a> and <a href="/wiki/Irish_annals" title="Irish annals">annals</a>, which have considerable value but lack the <a href="/wiki/Empiricism" title="Empiricism">empiricism</a> scholars depend on today.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Name">Name</span></h3>
<p>The only name that Patrick uses for himself in his own writings is <style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r920966791">.mw-parser-output span.smallcaps{font-variant:small-caps}.mw-parser-output span.smallcaps-smaller{font-size:85%}</style><span class="smallcaps"><a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/patricius" class="extiw" title="wikt:patricius">Pātricius</a></span> <small></small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="la-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Latin" title="Help:IPA/Latin">[paːˈtrɪ.ki.ʊs]</a></span>, which gives <a href="/wiki/Old_Irish_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Old Irish language">Old Irish</a>: <i lang="sga">Pátraic</i> <small></small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="ga-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Irish" title="Help:IPA/Irish">[ˈpˠaːd̪ˠɾˠəɟ]</a></span> and <a href="/wiki/Irish_language" title="Irish language">Irish</a>: <i lang="ga">Pádraig</i> (<small></small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="ga-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Irish" title="Help:IPA/Irish">[ˈpˠaːd̪ˠɾˠəɟ]</a></span> or <small></small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="ga-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Irish" title="Help:IPA/Irish">[ˈpˠɑːɾˠɪɟ]</a></span>); English <i>Patrick</i>; <a href="/wiki/Scottish_Gaelic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Scottish Gaelic language">Scottish Gaelic</a>: <i lang="gd">Pàdraig</i>; <a href="/wiki/Welsh_language" title="Welsh language">Welsh</a>: <i lang="cy">Padrig</i>; <a href="/wiki/Cornish_language" title="Cornish language">Cornish</a>: <i lang="kw">Petroc</i>.
</p><p>Hagiography records other names he is said to have borne. <a href="/wiki/T%C3%ADrech%C3%A1n" title="Tírechán">Tírechán</a>'s seventh-century <i>Collectanea</i> gives: "Magonus, that is, famous; Succetus, that is, god of war; Patricius, that is, father of the citizens; Cothirthiacus, because he served four houses of druids."<sup id="cite_ref-Dumville90_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dumville90-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> "Magonus" appears in the ninth century <i><a href="/wiki/Historia_Brittonum" title="Historia Brittonum">Historia Brittonum</a></i> as <i>Maun</i>, descending from British <i>*Magunos</i>, meaning "servant-lad".<sup id="cite_ref-Dumville90_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dumville90-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> "Succetus", which also appears in <a href="/wiki/Muirch%C3%BA_moccu_Machtheni" title="Muirchú moccu Machtheni">Muirchú moccu Machtheni</a>'s seventh century <i>Life</i> as <i>Sochet</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-Dumville90_12-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dumville90-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> is identified by Mac Neill as "a word of British origin meaning swineherd".<sup id="cite_ref-EMN_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EMN-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Cothirthiacus also appears as <i>Cothraige</i> in the 8th century biographical poem known as <i>Fiacc's Hymn</i> and a variety of other spellings elsewhere, and is taken to represent a <a href="/wiki/Primitive_Irish_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Primitive Irish language">Primitive Irish</a>: &#42;<i lang="pgl">Qatrikias</i>, although this is disputed. Harvey argues that <i>Cothraige</i> "has the form of a classic Old Irish tribal (and therefore place-) name", noting that <i>Ail Coithrigi</i> is a name for the <a href="/wiki/Rock_of_Cashel" title="Rock of Cashel">Rock of Cashel</a>, and the place-names <i>Cothrugu</i> and <i>Catrige</i> are attested in Counties <a href="/wiki/County_Antrim" title="County Antrim">Antrim</a> and <a href="/wiki/County_Carlow" title="County Carlow">Carlow</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Dating">Dating</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Gravestone_of_St._Patrick,_Downpatrick_2018-07-25.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Gravestone_of_St._Patrick%2C_Downpatrick_2018-07-25.jpg/220px-Gravestone_of_St._Patrick%2C_Downpatrick_2018-07-25.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="265" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Gravestone_of_St._Patrick%2C_Downpatrick_2018-07-25.jpg/330px-Gravestone_of_St._Patrick%2C_Downpatrick_2018-07-25.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Gravestone_of_St._Patrick%2C_Downpatrick_2018-07-25.jpg/440px-Gravestone_of_St._Patrick%2C_Downpatrick_2018-07-25.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4906" data-file-height="5903" /></a><figcaption>The reputed burial place of Saint Patrick in <a href="/wiki/Downpatrick" title="Downpatrick">Downpatrick</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The dates of Patrick's life are uncertain; there are conflicting traditions regarding the year of his death. His own writings provide no evidence for any dating more precise than the 5th century generally. His Biblical quotations are a mixture of the <a href="/wiki/Vetus_Latina" title="Vetus Latina">Old Latin</a> version and the <a href="/wiki/Vulgate" title="Vulgate">Vulgate</a>, completed in the early 5th century, suggesting he was writing "at the point of transition from Old Latin to Vulgate",<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> although it is possible the Vulgate readings may have been added later, replacing earlier readings.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> The <i>Letter to Coroticus</i> implies that the <a href="/wiki/Franks" title="Franks">Franks</a> were still pagans at the time of writing:<sup id="cite_ref-Ó_Cróinín_1995,_p._26_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ó_Cróinín_1995,_p._26-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> their conversion to Christianity is dated to the period 496–508.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Irish_annals" title="Irish annals">Irish annals</a> for the fifth century date Patrick's arrival in Ireland at 432, but they were compiled in the mid 6th century at the earliest.<sup id="cite_ref-Ó_Cróinín_1995,_p._26_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ó_Cróinín_1995,_p._26-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> The date 432 was probably chosen to minimise the contribution of <a href="/wiki/Palladius_(bishop_of_Ireland)" title="Palladius (bishop of Ireland)">Palladius</a>, who was known to have been sent to Ireland in 431, and maximise that of Patrick.<sup id="cite_ref-Byrne78–79_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Byrne78–79-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> A variety of dates are given for his death. In 457 "the elder Patrick" (<a href="/wiki/Irish_language" title="Irish language">Irish</a>: <i lang="ga">Patraic Sen</i>) is said to have died: this may refer to the death of Palladius, who according to the <i><a href="/wiki/Book_of_Armagh" title="Book of Armagh">Book of Armagh</a></i> was also called Patrick.<sup id="cite_ref-Byrne78–79_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Byrne78–79-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> In 461/2 the annals say that "Here some record the repose of Patrick";<sup id="cite_ref-Henny_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Henny-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 19">&#58;&#8202;19&#8202;</span></sup> in 492/3 they record the death of "Patrick, the arch-apostle (or archbishop and apostle) of the Scoti", on 17 March, at the age of 120.<sup id="cite_ref-Henny_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Henny-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 31">&#58;&#8202;31&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>While some modern historians<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> accept the earlier date of <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;460</span> for Patrick's death, scholars of early Irish history tend to prefer a later date, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;493</span>. Supporting the later date, the annals record that in 553 "the relics of Patrick were placed <i>sixty years after his death</i> in a shrine by <a href="/wiki/Colum_Cille" class="mw-redirect" title="Colum Cille">Colum Cille</a>" (emphasis added).<sup id="cite_ref-Paor121-122_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Paor121-122-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> The death of Patrick's disciple <a href="/wiki/Mochta" title="Mochta">Mochta</a> is dated in the annals to 535 or 537,<sup id="cite_ref-Paor121-122_22-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Paor121-122-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> and the early hagiographies "all bring Patrick into contact with persons whose obits occur at the end of the fifth century or the beginning of the sixth".<sup id="cite_ref-Byrne80_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Byrne80-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> However, <a href="/wiki/E._A._Thompson" title="E. A. Thompson">E. A. Thompson</a> argues that none of the dates given for Patrick's death in the Annals are reliable.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> A recent biography argues that a late fifth-century date for the saint is not impossible.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 34–35">&#58;&#8202;34–35&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Life">Life</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:End.of.Roman.rule.in.Britain.383.410.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/End.of.Roman.rule.in.Britain.383.410.jpg/220px-End.of.Roman.rule.in.Britain.383.410.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="274" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/End.of.Roman.rule.in.Britain.383.410.jpg/330px-End.of.Roman.rule.in.Britain.383.410.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/End.of.Roman.rule.in.Britain.383.410.jpg/440px-End.of.Roman.rule.in.Britain.383.410.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2582" data-file-height="3220" /></a><figcaption>Late Roman Britain</figcaption></figure>
<p>Patrick was born at the <a href="/wiki/End_of_Roman_rule_in_Britain" title="End of Roman rule in Britain">end of Roman rule in Britain</a>. His birthplace is not known with any certainty; some traditions place it in what is now England—one identifying it as Glannoventa (modern <a href="/wiki/Ravenglass" title="Ravenglass">Ravenglass</a> in <a href="/wiki/Cumbria" title="Cumbria">Cumbria</a>). In 1981, Thomas argued at length for the areas of <a href="/wiki/Birdoswald" title="Birdoswald">Birdoswald</a>, twenty miles (32&#160;km) east of Carlisle on <a href="/wiki/Hadrian%27s_Wall" title="Hadrian&#39;s Wall">Hadrian's Wall</a>. <a href="#CITEREFThomas1981">Thomas 1981</a>, pp.&#160;310–14. In 1993, Paor glossed it as "[probably near] <a href="/wiki/Carlisle,_Cumbria" class="mw-redirect" title="Carlisle, Cumbria">Carlisle</a>". There is a Roman town known as <a href="/wiki/Bannaventa" title="Bannaventa">Bannaventa</a> in Northamptonshire, which is phonically similar to the Bannavem Taburniae mentioned in Patrick’s confession, but this is probably too far from the sea.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Claims have also been advanced for locations in present-day Scotland, with the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Encyclopedia" title="Catholic Encyclopedia">Catholic Encyclopedia</a> stating that Patrick was born in <a href="/wiki/Old_Kilpatrick" title="Old Kilpatrick">Kilpatrick</a>, Scotland,<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>. In 1926 Eoin MacNeill also advanced a claim for <a href="/wiki/Glamorgan" title="Glamorgan">Glamorgan</a> in south Wales,<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> possibly the village of <a href="/wiki/Banwen" title="Banwen">Banwen</a>, in the Upper Dulais Valley, which was the location of a Roman marching camp.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Patrick's father, Calpurnius, is described as a <a href="/wiki/Decurion_(administrative)" class="mw-redirect" title="Decurion (administrative)">decurion</a> (Senator and tax collector) of an unspecified <a href="/wiki/End_of_Roman_rule_in_Britain" title="End of Roman rule in Britain">Romano-British city</a>, and as a <a href="/wiki/Deacon" title="Deacon">deacon</a>; his grandfather Potitus was a <a href="/wiki/Priest" title="Priest">priest</a> from Bonaven Tabernia.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> However, Patrick's confession states he was not an active believer in his youth, and considered himself in that period to be "idle and callow".<sup id="cite_ref-:1_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to the <i>Confession of Saint Patrick</i>, at the age of sixteen he was captured by a group of Irish pirates, from his family's Villa at "Bannavem Taburniae".<sup id="cite_ref-ccel.org_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ccel.org-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> They took him to Ireland where he was enslaved and held captive for six years. Patrick writes in the <i>Confession</i><sup id="cite_ref-ccel.org_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ccel.org-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> that the time he spent in captivity was critical to his spiritual development. He explains that the Lord had mercy on his youth and ignorance, and afforded him the opportunity to be forgiven his sins and convert to Christianity. 
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/D%C3%A1l_Riata" title="Dál Riata">Dál Riata</a> raiders who kidnapped him introduced him to the Irish culture that would define his life and <a href="/wiki/Reputation" title="Reputation">reputation</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> While in captivity, he worked as a shepherd and strengthened his relationship with God through prayer, eventually leading him to convert to Christianity.<sup id="cite_ref-ccel.org_33-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ccel.org-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After six years of captivity he heard a voice telling him that he would soon go home, and then that his ship was ready. Fleeing his master, he travelled to a port, two hundred miles away,<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> where he found a ship and with difficulty persuaded the captain to take him. After three days' sailing, they landed, presumably in Britain, and apparently all left the ship, walking for 28 days in a "wilderness" and becoming faint from hunger. Patrick's account of his escape from slavery and return home to Britain is recounted in his <i>Declaration</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> After Patrick prayed for sustenance, they encountered a herd of <a href="/wiki/Wild_boar" title="Wild boar">wild boar</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> since this was shortly after Patrick had urged them to put their faith in God, his prestige in the group was greatly increased. After various adventures, he returned home to his family, now in his early twenties.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> After returning home to Britain, Patrick continued to study Christianity.
</p><p>
Patrick recounts that he had a vision a few years after returning home:</p><blockquote><p>I saw a man coming, as it were from Ireland. His name was Victoricus, and he carried many letters, and he gave me one of them. I read the heading: "The Voice of the Irish". As I began the letter, I imagined in that moment that I heard the voice of those very people who were near the wood of <a href="/wiki/Foclut" title="Foclut">Foclut</a>, which is beside the western sea—and they cried out, as with one voice: "We appeal to you, holy servant boy, to come and walk among us."<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>A.B.E. Hood suggests that the Victoricus of St. Patrick's vision may be identified with Saint <a href="/wiki/Victricius" title="Victricius">Victricius</a>, bishop of <a href="/wiki/Rouen" title="Rouen">Rouen</a> in the late fourth century, who had visited Britain in an official capacity in 396.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> However, Ludwig Bieler disagrees.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Patrick studied in Europe principally at <a href="/wiki/Auxerre" title="Auxerre">Auxerre</a>, but is thought to have visited the <a href="/wiki/Marmoutier_Abbey,_Tours" title="Marmoutier Abbey, Tours">Marmoutier Abbey, Tours</a> and to have received the <a href="/wiki/Tonsure" title="Tonsure">tonsure</a> at <a href="/wiki/L%C3%A9rins_Abbey" title="Lérins Abbey">Lérins Abbey</a>. <a href="/wiki/Saint_Germanus_of_Auxerre" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Germanus of Auxerre">Saint Germanus of Auxerre</a>, a bishop of the <a href="/wiki/Western_Christianity" title="Western Christianity">Western Church</a>, ordained him to the priesthood.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Moran_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Moran-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ireland%27s_crown_of_thorns_and_roses;_or,_The_best_of_her_history_by_the_best_of_her_writers,_a_series_of_historical_narratives_that_read_as_entertainingly_as_a_novel_(1904)_(14754489376).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/Ireland%27s_crown_of_thorns_and_roses%3B_or%2C_The_best_of_her_history_by_the_best_of_her_writers%2C_a_series_of_historical_narratives_that_read_as_entertainingly_as_a_novel_%281904%29_%2814754489376%29.jpg/220px-thumbnail.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="301" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/Ireland%27s_crown_of_thorns_and_roses%3B_or%2C_The_best_of_her_history_by_the_best_of_her_writers%2C_a_series_of_historical_narratives_that_read_as_entertainingly_as_a_novel_%281904%29_%2814754489376%29.jpg/330px-thumbnail.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/Ireland%27s_crown_of_thorns_and_roses%3B_or%2C_The_best_of_her_history_by_the_best_of_her_writers%2C_a_series_of_historical_narratives_that_read_as_entertainingly_as_a_novel_%281904%29_%2814754489376%29.jpg/440px-thumbnail.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1920" data-file-height="2628" /></a><figcaption>"Patrick going to <a href="/wiki/Hill_of_Tara" title="Hill of Tara">Tara</a>", illustration from a 1904 book</figcaption></figure>
<p>Acting on his vision, Patrick returned to Ireland as a Christian missionary.<sup id="cite_ref-ccel.org_33-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ccel.org-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="/wiki/J._B._Bury" title="J. B. Bury">J. B. Bury</a>, his landing place was <a href="/wiki/Wicklow" title="Wicklow">Wicklow</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_Wicklow" title="County Wicklow">County Wicklow</a>, at the mouth of the river Inver-dea, which is now called the Vartry.<sup id="cite_ref-Bury81_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bury81-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Bury suggests that Wicklow was also the port through which Patrick made his escape after his six years' captivity, though he offers only circumstantial evidence to support this.<sup id="cite_ref-Bury31_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bury31-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Tradition has it that Patrick was not welcomed by the locals and was forced to leave and seek a more welcoming landing place further north. He rested for some days at the islands off the Skerries coast, one of which still retains the name of Inis-Patrick. The first sanctuary dedicated by Patrick was at <a href="/wiki/Saul_Monastery" title="Saul Monastery">Saul</a>. Shortly thereafter Benin (or <a href="/wiki/Benignus_of_Armagh" title="Benignus of Armagh">Benignus</a>), son of the chieftain Secsnen, joined Patrick's group.<sup id="cite_ref-Moran_42-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Moran-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Much of the <i>Declaration</i> concerns charges made against Patrick by his fellow Christians at a trial. What these charges were, he does not say explicitly, but he writes that he returned the gifts which wealthy women gave him, did not accept payment for <a href="/wiki/Baptism" title="Baptism">baptisms</a>, nor for <a href="/wiki/Ordain" class="mw-redirect" title="Ordain">ordaining</a> priests, and indeed paid for many gifts to kings and judges, and paid for the sons of chiefs to accompany him. It is concluded, therefore, that he was accused of some sort of financial impropriety, and perhaps of having obtained his bishopric in Ireland with personal gain in mind.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The condemnation might have contributed to his decision to return to Ireland. According to Patrick's most recent biographer, Roy Flechner, the <i>Confessio</i> was written in part as a defence against his detractors, who did not believe that he was taken to Ireland as a slave, despite Patrick's vigorous insistence that he was.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Patrick eventually returned to Ireland, probably settling in the west of the island, where, in later life, he became a bishop and ordained subordinate clerics.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Carlow_Cathedral_St_Patrick_Preaching_to_the_Kings_2009_09_03.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Carlow_Cathedral_St_Patrick_Preaching_to_the_Kings_2009_09_03.jpg/220px-Carlow_Cathedral_St_Patrick_Preaching_to_the_Kings_2009_09_03.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="120" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Carlow_Cathedral_St_Patrick_Preaching_to_the_Kings_2009_09_03.jpg/330px-Carlow_Cathedral_St_Patrick_Preaching_to_the_Kings_2009_09_03.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Carlow_Cathedral_St_Patrick_Preaching_to_the_Kings_2009_09_03.jpg/440px-Carlow_Cathedral_St_Patrick_Preaching_to_the_Kings_2009_09_03.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2073" data-file-height="1128" /></a><figcaption>Stained glass window in <a href="/wiki/Carlow_Cathedral" class="mw-redirect" title="Carlow Cathedral">Carlow Cathedral</a>, showing St Patrick preaching to Irish kings.</figcaption></figure><p>
From this same evidence, something can be seen of Patrick's mission. He writes that he "baptised thousands of people",<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> even planning to convert his slavers.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> He ordained priests to lead the new Christian communities. He converted wealthy women, some of whom became <a href="/wiki/Nun" title="Nun">nuns</a> in the face of family opposition. He also dealt with the sons of kings, converting them too.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> The <i>Confessio</i> is generally vague about the details of his work in Ireland, though giving some specific instances. This is partly because, as he says at points, he was writing for a local audience of Christians who knew him and his work. There are several mentions of travelling around the island, and of sometimes difficult interactions with the ruling elite. He does claim of the Irish: </p><blockquote><p>Never before did they know of God except to serve idols and unclean things. But now, they have become the people of the Lord, and are called children of God. The sons and daughters of the leaders of the Irish are seen to be monks and virgins of Christ!<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote><p> Patrick's position as a foreigner in Ireland was not an easy one. His refusal to accept gifts from kings placed him outside the normal ties of kinship, fosterage and affinity. Legally he was without protection, and he says that he was on one occasion beaten, robbed of all he had, and put in chains, perhaps awaiting execution.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Patrick says that he was also "many years later" a captive for 60 days, without giving details.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Murchiú's life of Saint Patrick contains a supposed prophecy by the <a href="/wiki/Druid" title="Druid">druids</a> which gives an impression of how Patrick and other Christian missionaries were seen by those hostile to them:
</p>
<blockquote><div class="poem">
<p>Across the sea will come <a href="/wiki/Adze" title="Adze">Adze</a>-head,<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> crazed in the head,<br />
his cloak with hole for the head, his stick bent in the head.<br />
He will chant impieties from a table in the front of his house;<br />
all his people will answer: "so be it, so be it."<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
</div></blockquote>
<p>The second piece of evidence that comes from Patrick's life is the <i>Letter to Coroticus</i> or <i>Letter to the Soldiers of Coroticus</i>, written after a first remonstrance was received with ridicule and insult. In this, Patrick writes<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> an <a href="/wiki/Open_letter" title="Open letter">open letter</a> announcing that he has <a href="/wiki/Excommunicate" class="mw-redirect" title="Excommunicate">excommunicated</a> Coroticus because he had taken some of Patrick's converts into slavery while raiding in Ireland. The letter describes the followers of Coroticus as "fellow citizens of the devils" and "associates of the Scots [of <a href="/wiki/Dalriada" class="mw-redirect" title="Dalriada">Dalriada</a> and later Argyll] and <a href="/wiki/Apostasy" title="Apostasy">Apostate</a> <a href="/wiki/Picts" title="Picts">Picts</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Based largely on an eighth-century <a href="/wiki/Gloss_(margin_text)" class="mw-redirect" title="Gloss (margin text)">gloss</a>, Coroticus is taken to be King <a href="/wiki/Ceretic_of_Alt_Clut" class="mw-redirect" title="Ceretic of Alt Clut">Ceretic of Alt Clut</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Thompson however proposed that based on the evidence it is more likely that Coroticus was a British Roman living in Ireland.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> It has been suggested that it was the sending of this letter which provoked the trial which Patrick mentions in the <i>Confession</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Seventh-century_writings">Seventh-century writings</span></h2>
<p>An early document which is silent concerning Patrick is the letter of <a href="/wiki/Columbanus" title="Columbanus">Columbanus</a> to <a href="/wiki/Pope_Boniface_IV" title="Pope Boniface IV">Pope Boniface IV</a> of about 613. Columbanus writes that Ireland's Christianity "was first handed to us by you, the successors of the holy apostles", apparently referring to <a href="/wiki/Palladius_(bishop_of_Ireland)" title="Palladius (bishop of Ireland)">Palladius</a> only, and ignoring Patrick.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Writing on the <a href="/wiki/Easter_controversy" title="Easter controversy">Easter controversy</a> in 632 or 633, Cummian—it is uncertain whether this is <a href="/wiki/Cumm%C3%A9ne_Fota" title="Cumméne Fota">Cumméne Fota</a>, associated with <a href="/wiki/Clonfert" title="Clonfert">Clonfert</a>, or <a href="/wiki/Cumm%C3%A9ne_Find" title="Cumméne Find">Cumméne Find</a>—does refer to Patrick, calling him "our papa"; that is, pope or <a href="/wiki/Primate_(bishop)" title="Primate (bishop)">primate</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Two works by late seventh-century <a href="/wiki/Hagiographer" class="mw-redirect" title="Hagiographer">hagiographers</a> of Patrick have survived. These are the writings of Tírechán and the <i>Vita sancti Patricii</i> of Muirchú moccu Machtheni.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Both writers relied upon an earlier work, now lost, the <i>Book of Ultán</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> This Ultán, probably the same person as <a href="/wiki/Ultan_of_Ardbraccan" title="Ultan of Ardbraccan">Ultan of Ardbraccan</a>, was Tírechán's foster-father. His obituary is given in the <i><a href="/wiki/Annals_of_Ulster" title="Annals of Ulster">Annals of Ulster</a></i> under the year 657.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> These works thus date from a century and a half after Patrick's death.
</p><p>Tírechán writes, "I found four names for Patrick written in the book of Ultán, bishop of the tribe of <a href="/wiki/Conchobar" title="Conchobar">Conchobar</a>: holy <i>Magonus</i> (that is, "famous"); <i>Succetus</i> (that is, the god of war); <i>Patricius</i> (that is, father of the citizens); <i>Cothirtiacus</i> (because he served four houses of druids)."<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Muirchu records much the same information, adding that "[h]is mother was named Concessa".<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> The name <i>Cothirtiacus</i>, however, is simply the Latinised form of <a href="/wiki/Old_Irish" title="Old Irish">Old Irish</a> <i>Cothraige</i>, which is the <a href="/wiki/Q-Celtic" class="mw-redirect" title="Q-Celtic">Q-Celtic</a> form of <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a> <i>Patricius</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Patrick portrayed by Tírechán and Muirchu is a martial figure, who contests with <a href="/wiki/Druid" title="Druid">druids</a>, overthrows pagan idols, and curses kings and kingdoms.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> On occasion, their accounts contradict Patrick's own writings: Tírechán states that Patrick accepted gifts from female converts although Patrick himself flatly denies this. However, the emphasis Tírechán and Muirchu placed on female converts, and in particular royal and noble women who became nuns, is thought to be a genuine insight into Patrick's work of conversion. Patrick also worked with the unfree and the poor, encouraging them to vows of monastic chastity. Tírechán's account suggests that many early Patrician churches were combined with nunneries founded by Patrick's noble female converts.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The martial Patrick found in Tírechán and Muirchu, and in later accounts, echoes similar figures found during the conversion of the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Empire" title="Roman Empire">Roman Empire</a> to Christianity. It may be doubted whether such accounts are an accurate representation of Patrick's time, although such violent events may well have occurred as Christians gained in strength and numbers.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Much of the detail supplied by Tírechán and Muirchu, in particular the churches established by Patrick, and the monasteries founded by his converts, may relate to the situation in the seventh century, when the churches which claimed ties to Patrick, and in particular <a href="/wiki/Armagh" title="Armagh">Armagh</a>, were expanding their influence throughout Ireland in competition with the church of <a href="/wiki/Kildare" title="Kildare">Kildare</a>. In the same period, <a href="/wiki/Wilfrid_II_(bishop_of_York)" title="Wilfrid II (bishop of York)">Wilfred</a>, <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_York" title="Archbishop of York">Archbishop of York</a>, claimed to speak, as <a href="/wiki/Metropolitan_archbishop" class="mw-redirect" title="Metropolitan archbishop">metropolitan archbishop</a>, "for all the northern part of Britain and of Ireland" at a council held in Rome in the time of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Agatho" title="Pope Agatho">Pope Agatho</a>, thus claiming jurisdiction over the Irish church.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Other presumed early materials include the <a href="/wiki/Irish_annals" title="Irish annals">Irish annals</a>, which contain records from the <a href="/wiki/Chronicle_of_Ireland" title="Chronicle of Ireland">Chronicle of Ireland</a>. These sources have <a href="/wiki/Conflation" title="Conflation">conflated</a> <a href="/wiki/Palladius_(bishop_of_Ireland)" title="Palladius (bishop of Ireland)">Palladius</a> and Patrick.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Another early document is the so-called <i>First Synod of Saint Patrick</i>. This is a seventh-century document, once, but no longer, taken as to contain a fifth-century original text. It apparently collects the results of several early synods, and represents an era when pagans were still a major force in Ireland. The introduction attributes it to Patrick, Auxilius, and Iserninus, a claim which "cannot be taken at face value."<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legends">Legends</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Patrick_uses_shamrock_in_an_illustrative_parable">Patrick uses shamrock in an illustrative parable</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Kilbennan_St._Benin%27s_Church_Window_St._Patrick_Detail_2010_09_16.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/Kilbennan_St._Benin%27s_Church_Window_St._Patrick_Detail_2010_09_16.jpg/170px-Kilbennan_St._Benin%27s_Church_Window_St._Patrick_Detail_2010_09_16.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="249" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/Kilbennan_St._Benin%27s_Church_Window_St._Patrick_Detail_2010_09_16.jpg/255px-Kilbennan_St._Benin%27s_Church_Window_St._Patrick_Detail_2010_09_16.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/Kilbennan_St._Benin%27s_Church_Window_St._Patrick_Detail_2010_09_16.jpg/340px-Kilbennan_St._Benin%27s_Church_Window_St._Patrick_Detail_2010_09_16.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="880" /></a><figcaption>Patrick depicted with shamrock in detail of stained glass window in St. Benin's Church, Kilbennan, County Galway, Ireland</figcaption></figure>
<p>Legend credits Patrick with teaching the Irish about the doctrine of the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Trinity" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Trinity">Holy Trinity</a> by showing people the <a href="/wiki/Shamrock" title="Shamrock">shamrock</a>, a three-leafed plant, using it to illustrate the Christian teaching of three persons in one God.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> The earliest written version of the story is given by the botanist <a href="/wiki/Caleb_Threlkeld" title="Caleb Threlkeld">Caleb Threlkeld</a> in his 1726 <i>Synopsis stirpium Hibernicarum</i>, but the earliest surviving records associating Patrick with the plant are coins depicting Patrick clutching a shamrock which were minted in the 1680s.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFlechner2019221_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlechner2019221-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In pagan Ireland, three was a significant number and the Irish had many <a href="/wiki/Triple_deity" title="Triple deity">triple deities</a>, a fact that may have aided Patrick in his <a href="/wiki/Evangelism" title="Evangelism">evangelisation</a> efforts when he "held up a shamrock and discoursed on the Christian Trinity".<sup id="cite_ref-Monaghan2009_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Monaghan2009-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Hegarty2012_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hegarty2012-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Patricia_Monaghan" title="Patricia Monaghan">Patricia Monaghan</a> says there is no evidence that the shamrock was sacred to the pagan Irish.<sup id="cite_ref-Monaghan2009_76-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Monaghan2009-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> However, <a href="/wiki/Jack_Santino" title="Jack Santino">Jack Santino</a> speculates that it may have represented the regenerative powers of nature, and was recast in a Christian context. <a href="/wiki/Icon" title="Icon">Icons</a> of St Patrick often depict the saint "with a <a href="/wiki/Christian_cross" title="Christian cross">cross</a> in one hand and a sprig of shamrocks in the other".<sup id="cite_ref-Santino1995_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Santino1995-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Roger Homan writes, "We can perhaps see St Patrick drawing upon the visual concept of the <i><a href="/wiki/Triple_spiral" class="mw-redirect" title="Triple spiral">triskele</a></i> when he uses the shamrock to explain the Trinity".<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Patrick_banishes_snakes_from_Ireland">Patrick banishes snakes from Ireland</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:STP-ELP.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/STP-ELP.jpg/170px-STP-ELP.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="272" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/STP-ELP.jpg/255px-STP-ELP.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/STP-ELP.jpg/340px-STP-ELP.jpg 2x" data-file-width="727" data-file-height="1164" /></a><figcaption>Patrick banishing the snakes</figcaption></figure>
<p>Ireland was well-known to be a land without snakes, and this was noted as early as the third century by <a href="/wiki/Gaius_Julius_Solinus" title="Gaius Julius Solinus">Gaius Julius Solinus</a>, but later legend credited Patrick with banishing snakes from the island. The earliest text to mention an Irish saint banishing snakes from Ireland is in fact the Life of Saint <a href="/wiki/Columba" title="Columba">Columba</a> (chapter 3.23), written in the late seventh or early eighth century.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> The earliest writings about Patrick ridding Ireland of snakes are  by <a href="/wiki/Jocelyn_of_Furness" title="Jocelyn of Furness">Jocelyn of Furness</a> in the late twelfth century,<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> who says that Patrick chased them into the sea after they attacked him during his fast on a mountain.<sup id="cite_ref-news.nationalgeographic.com_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-news.nationalgeographic.com-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>  <a href="/wiki/Gerald_of_Wales" title="Gerald of Wales">Gerald of Wales</a> also mentions the story in the early thirteenth century, but he is doubtful of its truthfulness.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>  The hagiographic theme of banishing snakes may draw on the Biblical account of the staff of the prophet <a href="/wiki/Moses" title="Moses">Moses</a>. In <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://bible.oremus.org/?passage=Exodus%207:8–13&amp;version=nrsv">Exodus 7:8–7:13</a>, Moses and Aaron use their staffs in their struggle with Pharaoh's sorcerers, the staffs of each side turning into snakes. Aaron's snake-staff prevails by consuming the other snakes.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/List_of_reptiles_of_Ireland#Order:_Squamata_(lizards,_snakes)" title="List of reptiles of Ireland">Post-glacial Ireland never had snakes</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-news.nationalgeographic.com_82-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-news.nationalgeographic.com-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> "At no time has there ever been any suggestion of snakes in Ireland, so [there was] nothing for St. Patrick to banish", says naturalist Nigel Monaghan, keeper of natural history at the National Museum of Ireland in Dublin, who has searched extensively through Irish fossil collections and records.<sup id="cite_ref-news.nationalgeographic.com_82-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-news.nationalgeographic.com-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Patrick.27s_fast_on_the_mountain"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Patrick's_fast_on_the_mountain">Patrick's fast on the mountain</span></h3>
<p><a href="/wiki/T%C3%ADrech%C3%A1n" title="Tírechán">Tírechán</a> wrote in the 7th century that Patrick spent forty days on the mountaintop of <a href="/wiki/Croagh_Patrick" title="Croagh Patrick">Cruachán Aigle</a>, as <a href="/wiki/Moses" title="Moses">Moses</a> did on <a href="/wiki/Mount_Sinai_(Bible)" title="Mount Sinai (Bible)">Mount Sinai</a>. The 9th century <i>Bethu Phátraic</i> says that Patrick was harassed by a flock of black demonic birds while on the peak, and he banished them into the hollow of Lugnademon ("hollow of the demons") by ringing his bell. Patrick ended his fast when God gave him the right to judge all the Irish at the <a href="/wiki/Last_Judgment" title="Last Judgment">Last Judgement</a>, and agreed to spare the land of Ireland from the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Revelation" title="Book of Revelation">final desolation</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> A later legend tells how Patrick was tormented on the mountain by a demonic female serpent named Corra or Caorthannach. Patrick is said to have banished the serpent into Lough Na Corra below the mountain, or into a hollow from which the lake burst forth.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> The mountain is now known as Croagh Patrick (Cruach Phádraig) after the saint.
</p>
<h3><span id="Patrick_and_D.C3.A1ire"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Patrick_and_Dáire">Patrick and Dáire</span></h3>
<p>According to tradition, Patrick founded his main church at <a href="/wiki/Armagh" title="Armagh">Armagh</a> (Ard Mhacha) in the year 445. <a href="/wiki/Muirch%C3%BA_moccu_Machtheni" title="Muirchú moccu Machtheni">Muirchú</a> writes that a pagan chieftain named <a href="/wiki/D%C3%A1ire" title="Dáire">Dáire</a> would not let Patrick build a church on the hill of Ard Mhacha, but instead gave him lower ground to the east. One day, Dáire's horses die after grazing on the church land. He tells his men to kill Patrick, but is himself struck down with illness. Dáire's men beg Patrick to heal him, and Patrick's holy water revives both Dáire and his horses. Dáire rewards Patrick with a great bronze cauldron and gave him the hill of Ard Mhacha to build a church, which eventually became the head church of Ireland. Dáire has similarities with <a href="/wiki/The_Dagda" title="The Dagda">the Dagda</a>, an Irish god who owns a cauldron of plenty.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In a later legend, the pagan chieftain is named <a href="/wiki/Crom_Dubh" title="Crom Dubh">Crom</a>. Patrick asks the chieftain for food, and Crom sends his bull, in the hope that it will drive off or kill Patrick. Instead, it meekly submits to Patrick, allowing itself to be slaughtered and eaten. Crom demands his bull be returned. Patrick has the bull's bones and hide put together and brings it back to life. In some versions, Crom is so impressed that he converts to Christianity, while in others he is killed by the bull. In parts of Ireland, <a href="/wiki/Lughnasa" class="mw-redirect" title="Lughnasa">Lughnasa</a> (1 August) is called 'Crom's Sunday' and the legend could recall <a href="/wiki/Bull_sacrifice" class="mw-redirect" title="Bull sacrifice">bull sacrifices</a> during the festival.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Patrick_speaks_with_ancient_Irish_ancestors">Patrick speaks with ancient Irish ancestors</span></h3>
<p>The twelfth-century work <i><a href="/wiki/Acallam_na_Sen%C3%B3rach" title="Acallam na Senórach">Acallam na Senórach</a></i> tells of Patrick being met by two ancient warriors, <a href="/wiki/Ca%C3%ADlte_mac_R%C3%B3n%C3%A1in" title="Caílte mac Rónáin">Caílte mac Rónáin</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ois%C3%ADn" title="Oisín">Oisín</a>, during his evangelical travels. The two were once members of <a href="/wiki/Fionn_mac_Cumhaill" title="Fionn mac Cumhaill">Fionn mac Cumhaill</a>'s warrior band the <a href="/wiki/Fianna" title="Fianna">Fianna</a>, and somehow survived to Patrick's time.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> In the work St. Patrick seeks to convert the warriors to Christianity, while they defend their pagan past. The heroic pagan lifestyle of the warriors, of fighting and feasting and living close to nature, is contrasted with the more peaceful, but unheroic and non-sensual life offered by Christianity.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (March 2018)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Patrick_and_the_innkeeper">Patrick and the innkeeper</span></h3>
<p>A much later legend tells of Patrick visiting an <a href="/wiki/Inn" title="Inn">inn</a> and chiding the innkeeper for being ungenerous with her guests. Patrick tells her that a demon is hiding in her cellar and being fattened by her dishonesty. He says that the only way to get rid of the demon is by mending her ways. Some time later, Patrick revisits the inn to find that the innkeeper is now serving her guests cups of whiskey filled to the brim. He praises her generosity and brings her to the cellar, where they find the demon withering away. It then flees in a flash of flame, and Patrick decrees that people should have a drink of whiskey on his feast day in memory of this. This is said to be the origin of "drowning the shamrock" on Saint Patrick's Day.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Battle_for_the_Body_of_St_Patrick">Battle for the Body of St Patrick</span></h3>
<p>According to the <i><a href="/wiki/Annals_of_the_Four_Masters" title="Annals of the Four Masters">Annals of the Four Masters</a></i>, an early-modern compilation of earlier annals, his corpse soon became an object of conflict in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_for_the_Body_of_Saint_Patrick" class="mw-redirect" title="Battle for the Body of Saint Patrick">Battle for the Body of Saint Patrick</a> (<span title="Irish-language text"><i lang="ga">Cath Coirp Naomh Padraic</i></span>):
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p> The <a href="/wiki/U%C3%AD_N%C3%A9ill" title="Uí Néill">Uí Néill</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Airg%C3%ADalla" title="Airgíalla">Airgíalla</a> attempted to bring it to Armagh; the <a href="/wiki/Ulaid" title="Ulaid">Ulaid</a> tried to keep it for themselves.</p><div class="paragraphbreak" style="margin-top:0.5em"></div><p>When the Uí Néill and the Airgíalla came to a certain water, the river swelled against them so that they were not able to cross it. When the flood had subsided the Ui Neill and the Ulaid united on terms of peace, to bring the body of Patrick with them. It appeared to each of them that each had the body conveying it to their respective territories. The body of Patrick was afterwards interred at <a href="/wiki/Downpatrick" title="Downpatrick">Dun Da Lethglas</a> with great honour and veneration; and during the twelve nights that the religious seniors were watching the body with psalms and hymns, it was not night in Magh Inis or the neighbouring lands, as they thought, but as if it were the full undarkened light of day.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Modern_theories">Modern theories</span></h2>
<h3><span id=".22Two_Patricks.22_theory"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="&quot;Two_Patricks&quot;_theory">"Two Patricks" theory</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Patrick%27s_mission_to_Ireland_from_the_Pope,_Kilkenny_cathedral.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Patrick%27s_mission_to_Ireland_from_the_Pope%2C_Kilkenny_cathedral.jpg/220px-Patrick%27s_mission_to_Ireland_from_the_Pope%2C_Kilkenny_cathedral.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Patrick%27s_mission_to_Ireland_from_the_Pope%2C_Kilkenny_cathedral.jpg/330px-Patrick%27s_mission_to_Ireland_from_the_Pope%2C_Kilkenny_cathedral.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Patrick%27s_mission_to_Ireland_from_the_Pope%2C_Kilkenny_cathedral.jpg/440px-Patrick%27s_mission_to_Ireland_from_the_Pope%2C_Kilkenny_cathedral.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="900" /></a><figcaption>Saint Patrick sent to Ireland by the Pope; wall mosaic in <a href="/wiki/St_Mary%27s_Cathedral,_Kilkenny" title="St Mary&#39;s Cathedral, Kilkenny">St Mary's Cathedral, Kilkenny</a>. Emphasising the supposed papal mission of Patrick would help lend credence to the Catholic teaching that the Irish church was always under Papal authority.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Irish academic <a href="/wiki/T._F._O%27Rahilly" title="T. F. O&#39;Rahilly">T. F. O'Rahilly</a> proposed the "Two Patricks" theory,<sup id="cite_ref-O&#39;Rahilly_1942_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-O&#39;Rahilly_1942-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> which suggests that many of the traditions later attached to Saint Patrick actually concerned the aforementioned <a href="/wiki/Palladius_(bishop_of_Ireland)" title="Palladius (bishop of Ireland)">Palladius</a>, who, according to <a href="/wiki/Prosper_of_Aquitaine" title="Prosper of Aquitaine">Prosper of Aquitaine</a>'s <i>Chronicle</i>, was sent by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Celestine_I" title="Pope Celestine I">Pope Celestine I</a> as the first bishop to Irish Christians in 431. Palladius was not the only early cleric in Ireland at this time. The Irish-born Saint <a href="/wiki/Ciar%C3%A1n_of_Saigir" title="Ciarán of Saigir">Ciarán of Saigir</a> lived in the later fourth century (352–402) and was the first bishop of <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_Ossory" class="mw-redirect" title="Diocese of Ossory">Ossory</a>. Ciaran, along with saints <a href="/wiki/Auxilius_of_Ireland" title="Auxilius of Ireland">Auxilius</a>, <a href="/wiki/Secundinus" title="Secundinus">Secundinus</a> and <a href="/wiki/Iserninus" title="Iserninus">Iserninus</a>, is also associated with early churches in <a href="/wiki/Munster" title="Munster">Munster</a> and <a href="/wiki/Leinster" title="Leinster">Leinster</a>. By this reading, <a href="/wiki/Palladius_(bishop_of_Ireland)" title="Palladius (bishop of Ireland)">Palladius</a> was active in Ireland until the 460s.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Prosper associates Palladius' appointment with the visits of <a href="/wiki/Germanus_of_Auxerre" title="Germanus of Auxerre">Germanus of Auxerre</a> to Britain to suppress <a href="/wiki/Pelagianism" title="Pelagianism">Pelagianism</a> and it has been suggested that Palladius and his colleagues were sent to Ireland to ensure that exiled Pelagians did not establish themselves among the Irish Christians. The appointment of Palladius and his fellow-bishops was not obviously a mission to convert the Irish, but more probably intended to minister to existing Christian communities in Ireland.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> The sites of churches associated with Palladius and his colleagues are close to royal centres of the period: Secundus is remembered by <a href="/wiki/Dunshaughlin" title="Dunshaughlin">Dunshaughlin</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_Meath" title="County Meath">County Meath</a>, close to the <a href="/wiki/Hill_of_Tara" title="Hill of Tara">Hill of Tara</a> which is associated with the <a href="/wiki/High_King_of_Ireland" title="High King of Ireland">High King of Ireland</a>; <a href="/wiki/Killashee,_County_Kildare" class="mw-redirect" title="Killashee, County Kildare">Killashee, County Kildare</a>, close to <a href="/wiki/Naas" title="Naas">Naas</a> with links with the <a href="/wiki/List_of_kings_of_Leinster" title="List of kings of Leinster">kings of Leinster</a>, is probably named for Auxilius. This activity was limited to the southern half of Ireland, and there is no evidence for them in <a href="/wiki/Ulster" title="Ulster">Ulster</a> or <a href="/wiki/Connacht" title="Connacht">Connacht</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although the evidence for contacts with <a href="/wiki/Gaul" title="Gaul">Gaul</a> is clear, the borrowings from <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a> into <a href="/wiki/Old_Irish" title="Old Irish">Old Irish</a> show that links with <a href="/wiki/Roman_Britain" title="Roman Britain">Roman Britain</a> were many.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> Iserninus, who appears to be of the generation of Palladius, is thought to have been a <a href="/wiki/Celtic_Britons" title="Celtic Britons">Briton</a>, and is associated with the lands of the <a href="/wiki/U%C3%AD_Ceinnselaig" title="Uí Ceinnselaig">Uí Ceinnselaig</a> in Leinster. The Palladian mission should not be contrasted with later "British" missions, but forms a part of them;<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> nor can the work of Palladius be uncritically equated with that of Saint Patrick, as was once traditional.<sup id="cite_ref-O&#39;Rahilly_1942_93-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-O&#39;Rahilly_1942-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Abduction_reinterpreted">Abduction reinterpreted</span></h3>
<p>According to Patrick's own account, it was Irish raiders who brought him to Ireland where he was enslaved and held captive for six years.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> However, a recent alternative interpretation of Patrick's departure to Ireland suggests that, as the son of a <a href="/wiki/Decurion_(administrative)" class="mw-redirect" title="Decurion (administrative)">decurion</a>, he would have been obliged by Roman law to serve on the town council (<a href="/wiki/Curia" title="Curia">curia</a>), but chose instead to abscond from the onerous obligations of this office by fleeing abroad, as many others in his position had done in what has become known as the 'flight of the <a href="/wiki/Curiales" title="Curiales">curiales</a>'.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> Roy Flechner also asserts the improbability of an escape from servitude and journey of the kind that Patrick purports to have undertaken. He also interprets the biblical allusions in Patrick's account (e.g. the theme of freedom after six years of servitude in Exod. 21:2 or Jer. 34:14), as implying parts of the account may not have been intended to be understood literally.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Sainthood_and_veneration">Sainthood and veneration</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Icon_of_Saint_Patrick,_Christ_the_Saviour_Church.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Icon_of_Saint_Patrick%2C_Christ_the_Saviour_Church.jpg/220px-Icon_of_Saint_Patrick%2C_Christ_the_Saviour_Church.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="314" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Icon_of_Saint_Patrick%2C_Christ_the_Saviour_Church.jpg/330px-Icon_of_Saint_Patrick%2C_Christ_the_Saviour_Church.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Icon_of_Saint_Patrick%2C_Christ_the_Saviour_Church.jpg/440px-Icon_of_Saint_Patrick%2C_Christ_the_Saviour_Church.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2109" data-file-height="3013" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Icon" title="Icon">Icon</a> of Saint Patrick from <a href="/wiki/Christ_the_Savior" class="mw-redirect" title="Christ the Savior">Christ the Savior</a> <a href="/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church" title="Russian Orthodox Church">Russian Orthodox Church</a>, <a href="/wiki/Wayne,_West_Virginia" title="Wayne, West Virginia">Wayne, West Virginia</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Armagh_St._Patrick%27s_Cathedral_of_the_Church_of_Ireland_East_Window_Detail_Saint_Patrick_2019_09_09.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Armagh_St._Patrick%27s_Cathedral_of_the_Church_of_Ireland_East_Window_Detail_Saint_Patrick_2019_09_09.jpg/220px-Armagh_St._Patrick%27s_Cathedral_of_the_Church_of_Ireland_East_Window_Detail_Saint_Patrick_2019_09_09.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="233" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Armagh_St._Patrick%27s_Cathedral_of_the_Church_of_Ireland_East_Window_Detail_Saint_Patrick_2019_09_09.jpg/330px-Armagh_St._Patrick%27s_Cathedral_of_the_Church_of_Ireland_East_Window_Detail_Saint_Patrick_2019_09_09.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Armagh_St._Patrick%27s_Cathedral_of_the_Church_of_Ireland_East_Window_Detail_Saint_Patrick_2019_09_09.jpg/440px-Armagh_St._Patrick%27s_Cathedral_of_the_Church_of_Ireland_East_Window_Detail_Saint_Patrick_2019_09_09.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3169" data-file-height="3352" /></a><figcaption>Stained glass window of St Patrick from the Protestant <a href="/wiki/St_Patrick%27s_Cathedral,_Armagh_(Church_of_Ireland)" title="St Patrick&#39;s Cathedral, Armagh (Church of Ireland)">Church of Ireland cathedral in Armagh</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>17 March, popularly known as <a href="/wiki/Saint_Patrick%27s_Day" title="Saint Patrick&#39;s Day">Saint Patrick's Day</a>, is believed to be his death date and is the date celebrated as his <a href="/wiki/Feast_Day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast Day">Feast Day</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> The day became a feast day in the Catholic Church due to the influence of the <a href="/wiki/Waterford" title="Waterford">Waterford</a>-born <a href="/wiki/Franciscan" class="mw-redirect" title="Franciscan">Franciscan</a> scholar <a href="/wiki/Luke_Wadding" title="Luke Wadding">Luke Wadding</a>, as a member of the commission for the reform of the <a href="/wiki/Breviary" title="Breviary">Breviary</a> in the early part of the 17th century.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>For most of Christianity's first thousand years, canonisations were done on the diocesan or regional level. Relatively soon after the death of people considered very holy, the local Church affirmed that they could be liturgically celebrated as saints. As a result, Patrick has never been formally <a href="/wiki/Canonisation" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonisation">canonised</a> by a pope (common before <a href="/wiki/Christianity_in_the_10th_century" title="Christianity in the 10th century">10th century</a>); nevertheless, various Christian churches declare that he is a saint in Heaven (see <a href="/wiki/List_of_Saints" class="mw-redirect" title="List of Saints">List of Saints</a>). He is still widely venerated in Ireland and elsewhere today.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Patrick is honoured with a <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> on the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church_in_the_United_States_of_America)" class="mw-redirect" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church in the United States of America)">liturgical calendar of the Episcopal Church (USA)</a> and with a commemoration on the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Lutheran)" title="Calendar of saints (Lutheran)">calendar</a> of <a href="/wiki/Evangelical_Lutheran_Worship" title="Evangelical Lutheran Worship">Evangelical Lutheran Worship</a>, both on 17 March. Patrick is also <a href="/wiki/Veneration" title="Veneration">venerated</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a> as a pre-Schism Western saint, especially among Orthodox Christians living in Ireland and the <a href="/wiki/Anglosphere" title="Anglosphere">Anglosphere</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> as is usual with saints, there are Orthodox <a href="/wiki/Icon" title="Icon">icons</a> dedicated to him.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Saint Patrick remains a recurring figure in <a href="/wiki/Folk_Christianity" class="mw-redirect" title="Folk Christianity">Folk Christianity</a> and Irish folktales.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Patrick is said to be buried at <a href="/wiki/Down_Cathedral" title="Down Cathedral">Down Cathedral</a> in <a href="/wiki/Downpatrick" title="Downpatrick">Downpatrick</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_Down" title="County Down">County Down</a>, alongside <a href="/wiki/Saint_Brigid" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Brigid">Saint Brigid</a> and <a href="/wiki/Saint_Columba" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Columba">Saint Columba</a>, although this has never been proven. <a href="/wiki/Saint_Patrick_Visitor_Centre" title="Saint Patrick Visitor Centre">Saint Patrick Visitor Centre</a> is a modern exhibition complex located in Downpatrick and is a permanent interpretative exhibition centre featuring interactive displays on the life and story of Patrick. It provides the only permanent exhibition centre in the world devoted to Patrick.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Patrick is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">remembered</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> with a <a href="/wiki/Lesser_Festival_(Anglicanism)" title="Lesser Festival (Anglicanism)">Lesser Festival</a> on <a href="/wiki/March_17" title="March 17">17 March</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 9 March 2017 his name was added to <a href="/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church" title="Russian Orthodox Church">Russian Orthodox Church</a> <a href="/wiki/Menologium" title="Menologium">calendar</a> by <a href="/wiki/Holy_Synod_of_the_Russian_Orthodox_Church" title="Holy Synod of the Russian Orthodox Church">Holy Synod of the Russian Orthodox Church</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:left;"></div>
<h3><span id="Saint_Patrick.27s_Breastplate"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Saint_Patrick's_Breastplate"><i>Saint Patrick's Breastplate</i></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Saint_Patrick%27s_Breastplate" title="Saint Patrick&#39;s Breastplate">Saint Patrick's Breastplate</a></div>
<p>Saint Patrick's Breastplate is a <i><a href="/wiki/Lorica_(prayer)" title="Lorica (prayer)">lorica</a></i>, or hymn, which is attributed to Patrick during his Irish ministry in the 5th century.
</p>
<h3><span id="Saint_Patrick.27s_crosses"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Saint_Patrick's_crosses">Saint Patrick's crosses</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/List_of_Saint_Patrick%27s_crosses" title="List of Saint Patrick&#39;s crosses">List of Saint Patrick's crosses</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Goleen_Church_of_Our_Lady,_Star_of_the_Sea,_and_St._Patrick_North_Wall_Fourth_Window_Saint_Patrick_Detail_2009_09_10.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/Goleen_Church_of_Our_Lady%2C_Star_of_the_Sea%2C_and_St._Patrick_North_Wall_Fourth_Window_Saint_Patrick_Detail_2009_09_10.jpg/170px-Goleen_Church_of_Our_Lady%2C_Star_of_the_Sea%2C_and_St._Patrick_North_Wall_Fourth_Window_Saint_Patrick_Detail_2009_09_10.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="346" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/Goleen_Church_of_Our_Lady%2C_Star_of_the_Sea%2C_and_St._Patrick_North_Wall_Fourth_Window_Saint_Patrick_Detail_2009_09_10.jpg/255px-Goleen_Church_of_Our_Lady%2C_Star_of_the_Sea%2C_and_St._Patrick_North_Wall_Fourth_Window_Saint_Patrick_Detail_2009_09_10.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/Goleen_Church_of_Our_Lady%2C_Star_of_the_Sea%2C_and_St._Patrick_North_Wall_Fourth_Window_Saint_Patrick_Detail_2009_09_10.jpg/340px-Goleen_Church_of_Our_Lady%2C_Star_of_the_Sea%2C_and_St._Patrick_North_Wall_Fourth_Window_Saint_Patrick_Detail_2009_09_10.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1068" data-file-height="2174" /></a><figcaption>Patrick showing cross pattée on his robes</figcaption></figure>
<p>There are two main types of crosses associated with Patrick, the <a href="/wiki/Cross_patt%C3%A9e" title="Cross pattée">cross pattée</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Cross_Saltire" class="mw-redirect" title="Cross Saltire">Saltire</a>. The cross pattée is the more traditional association, while the association with the saltire dates from 1783 and the Order of St. Patrick.
</p><p>The cross pattée has long been associated with Patrick, for reasons that are uncertain. One possible reason is that bishops' mitres in <a href="/wiki/Ecclesiastical_heraldry" title="Ecclesiastical heraldry">Ecclesiastical heraldry</a> often appear surmounted by a cross pattée.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> An example of this can be seen on the old crest of the <a href="/wiki/Patrician_Brothers" title="Patrician Brothers">Brothers of St. Patrick</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> As Patrick was the founding bishop of the Irish church, the symbol may have become associated with him. Patrick is traditionally portrayed in the vestments of a bishop, and his mitre and garments are often decorated with a cross pattée.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The cross pattée retains its link to Patrick to the present day. For example, it appears on the coat of arms of both the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Armagh" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Armagh">Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Armagh</a><sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/Archdiocese_of_Armagh_(Church_of_Ireland)" class="mw-redirect" title="Archdiocese of Armagh (Church of Ireland)">Church of Ireland Archdiocese of Armagh</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> This is on account of Patrick being regarded as the first bishop of the Diocese of Armagh. It is also used by <a href="/wiki/Down_District_Council" title="Down District Council">Down District Council</a> which has its headquarters in <a href="/wiki/Downpatrick" title="Downpatrick">Downpatrick</a>, the reputed burial place of Patrick.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Saint_Patrick%27s_Saltire" title="Saint Patrick&#39;s Saltire">Saint Patrick's Saltire</a> is a red <a href="/wiki/Saltire" title="Saltire">saltire</a> on a white field. It is used in the insignia of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Saint_Patrick" class="mw-redirect" title="Order of Saint Patrick">Order of Saint Patrick</a>, established in 1783, and after the <a href="/wiki/Acts_of_Union_1800" title="Acts of Union 1800">Acts of Union 1800</a> it was combined with the <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_England" title="Flag of England">Saint George's Cross of England</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_Scotland" title="Flag of Scotland">Saint Andrew's Cross of Scotland</a> to form the <a href="/wiki/Union_Flag" class="mw-redirect" title="Union Flag">Union Flag</a> of the <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland" title="United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland">United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland</a>. A saltire was intermittently used as a symbol of Ireland from the seventeenth century, but without reference to Patrick.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Traditional-irish-stipatricksidayibadges.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Photograph of eight home-made badges composed of variously coloured crosses and saltires." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5d/Traditional-irish-stipatricksidayibadges.jpg/240px-Traditional-irish-stipatricksidayibadges.jpg" decoding="async" width="240" height="80" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5d/Traditional-irish-stipatricksidayibadges.jpg/360px-Traditional-irish-stipatricksidayibadges.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5d/Traditional-irish-stipatricksidayibadges.jpg/480px-Traditional-irish-stipatricksidayibadges.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="334" /></a><figcaption>Traditional Saint Patrick's Day badges from the early twentieth century, from the <a href="/wiki/Museum_of_Country_Life" class="mw-redirect" title="Museum of Country Life">Museum of Country Life</a>, Castlebar</figcaption></figure>
<p>It was formerly a common custom to wear a cross made of paper or ribbon on <a href="/wiki/St_Patrick%27s_Day" class="mw-redirect" title="St Patrick&#39;s Day">St Patrick's Day</a>.
