Surviving examples of such badges come in many colours<sup id="cite_ref-hm40_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hm40-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> and they were worn upright rather than as saltires.<sup id="cite_ref-morley_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-morley-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Thomas Dinely, an English traveller in Ireland in 1681, remarked that "the Irish of all stations and condicõns were crosses in their hatts, some of pins, some of green ribbon."<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Jonathan_Swift" title="Jonathan Swift">Jonathan Swift</a>, writing to "<a href="/wiki/Esther_Johnson" title="Esther Johnson">Stella</a>" of Saint Patrick's Day 1713, said "<a href="/wiki/The_Mall,_London" title="The Mall, London">the Mall</a> was so full of crosses that I thought all the world was Irish".<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> In the 1740s, the badges pinned were multicoloured interlaced fabric.<sup id="cite_ref-badges_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-badges-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> In the 1820s, they were only worn by children, with simple multicoloured daisy patterns.<sup id="cite_ref-badges_126-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-badges-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> In the 1890s, they were almost extinct, and a simple green <a href="/wiki/Greek_cross" class="mw-redirect" title="Greek cross">Greek cross</a> inscribed in a circle of paper (similar to the Ballina crest pictured).<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> <i><a href="/wiki/The_Irish_Times" title="The Irish Times">The Irish Times</a></i> in 1935 reported they were still sold in poorer parts of Dublin, but fewer than those of previous years "some in velvet or embroidered silk or poplin, with the gold paper cross entwined with shamrocks and ribbons".<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Saint_Patrick.27s_Bell"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Saint_Patrick's_Bell">Saint Patrick's Bell</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Bell_of_Saint_Patrick_Shrine_MET_tem07651s1.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/The_Bell_of_Saint_Patrick_Shrine_MET_tem07651s1.jpg/170px-The_Bell_of_Saint_Patrick_Shrine_MET_tem07651s1.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/The_Bell_of_Saint_Patrick_Shrine_MET_tem07651s1.jpg/255px-The_Bell_of_Saint_Patrick_Shrine_MET_tem07651s1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/The_Bell_of_Saint_Patrick_Shrine_MET_tem07651s1.jpg/340px-The_Bell_of_Saint_Patrick_Shrine_MET_tem07651s1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1536" data-file-height="2048" /></a><figcaption>The Shrine of St. Patrick's Bell</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/National_Museum_of_Ireland" title="National Museum of Ireland">National Museum of Ireland</a> in Dublin possesses a bell (<i><span title="Irish-language text"><i lang="ga">Clog Phádraig</i></span></i>)<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> first mentioned, according to the <i><a href="/wiki/Annals_of_Ulster" title="Annals of Ulster">Annals of Ulster</a></i>, in the <i>Book of Cuanu</i> in the year 552. The bell was part of a collection of "relics of Patrick" removed from his tomb sixty years after his death by <a href="/wiki/Colum_Cille" class="mw-redirect" title="Colum Cille">Colum Cille</a> to be used as relics. The bell is described as "The Bell of the Testament", one of three relics of "precious minna" (extremely valuable items), of which the other two are described as Patrick's goblet and "The Angels Gospel". Colum Cille is described to have been under the direction of an "Angel" for whom he sent the goblet to <a href="/wiki/County_Down" title="County Down">Down</a>, the bell to <a href="/wiki/County_Armagh" title="County Armagh">Armagh</a>, and kept possession of the Angel's Gospel for himself. The name Angels Gospel is given to the book because it was supposed that Colum Cille received it from the angel's hand. A stir was caused in 1044 when two kings, in some dispute over the bell, went on spates of prisoner taking and cattle theft. The annals make one more apparent reference to the bell when chronicling a death, of 1356: "Solomon Ua Mellain, The Keeper of The Bell of the Testament, protector, rested in Christ."
</p><p>The bell was encased in a "bell shrine", a distinctive Irish type of <a href="/wiki/Reliquary" title="Reliquary">reliquary</a> made for it, as an inscription records, by King <a href="/wiki/Domnall_Ua_Lochlainn" title="Domnall Ua Lochlainn">Domnall Ua Lochlainn</a> sometime between 1091 and 1105. The shrine is an important example of the final, Viking-influenced, style of Irish <a href="/wiki/Celtic_art" title="Celtic art">Celtic art</a>, with intricate <a href="/wiki/Urnes_style" class="mw-redirect" title="Urnes style">Urnes style</a> decoration in gold and silver. The Gaelic inscription on the shrine also records the name of the maker "U INMAINEN" (which translates to "Noonan"), "who with his sons enriched/decorated it"; metalwork was often inscribed for remembrance.
</p><p>The bell itself is simple in design, hammered into shape with a small handle fixed to the top with rivets. Originally forged from iron, it has since been coated in bronze. The shrine is inscribed with three names, including King Domnall Ua Lochlainn's. The rear of the shrine, not intended to be seen, is decorated with crosses while the handle is decorated with, among other work, Celtic designs of birds. The bell is accredited with working a miracle in 1044,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify" title="Wikipedia:Please clarify"><span title="The text near this tag needs further explanation. (September 2020)">further explanation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> and having been coated in bronze to shield it from human eyes, for which it would be too holy. It measures 12.5&#160;×&#160;10&#160;cm at the base, 12.8&#160;×&#160;4&#160;cm at the shoulder, 16.5&#160;cm from base to shoulder, 3.3&#160;cm from shoulder to top of handle and weighs 1.7&#160;kg.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Saint_Patrick_and_Irish_identity">Saint Patrick and Irish identity</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:%22Erin_Go_Braugh._St._Patrick%27s_Greetings.%22.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/%22Erin_Go_Braugh._St._Patrick%27s_Greetings.%22.jpg/220px-%22Erin_Go_Braugh._St._Patrick%27s_Greetings.%22.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="350" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/%22Erin_Go_Braugh._St._Patrick%27s_Greetings.%22.jpg/330px-%22Erin_Go_Braugh._St._Patrick%27s_Greetings.%22.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/%22Erin_Go_Braugh._St._Patrick%27s_Greetings.%22.jpg/440px-%22Erin_Go_Braugh._St._Patrick%27s_Greetings.%22.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4108" data-file-height="6541" /></a><figcaption>A 1909 St Patrick's Day postcard with the Irish slogan "<a href="/wiki/Erin_go_bragh" title="Erin go bragh">Erin go bragh</a>" (Ireland Forever).</figcaption></figure>
<p>Patrick features in many stories in the Irish oral tradition and there are many customs connected with his feast day. The folklorist Jenny Butler discusses how these traditions have been given new layers of meaning over time while also becoming tied to Irish identity both in Ireland and abroad. The symbolic resonance of the Saint Patrick figure is complex and multifaceted, stretching from that of Christianity's arrival in Ireland to an identity that encompasses everything Irish. In some portrayals, the saint is symbolically synonymous with the Christian religion itself. There is also evidence of a combination of indigenous religious traditions with that of Christianity, which places St Patrick in the wider framework of <a href="/wiki/Cultural_hybridity" class="mw-redirect" title="Cultural hybridity">cultural hybridity</a>. Popular religious expression has this characteristic feature of merging elements of culture. Later in time, the saint becomes associated specifically with Catholic Ireland and synonymously with Irish national identity. Subsequently, Saint Patrick is a patriotic symbol along with the colour green and the shamrock. Saint Patrick's Day celebrations include many traditions that are known to be relatively recent historically, but have endured through time because of their association either with religious or national identity. They have persisted in such a way that they have become stalwart traditions, viewed as the strongest "Irish traditions".<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Places_associated_with_Saint_Patrick">Places associated with Saint Patrick</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Slemish_(8)_-_geograph.org.uk_-_834986.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Slemish_%288%29_-_geograph.org.uk_-_834986.jpg/220px-Slemish_%288%29_-_geograph.org.uk_-_834986.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="131" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Slemish_%288%29_-_geograph.org.uk_-_834986.jpg/330px-Slemish_%288%29_-_geograph.org.uk_-_834986.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Slemish_%288%29_-_geograph.org.uk_-_834986.jpg/440px-Slemish_%288%29_-_geograph.org.uk_-_834986.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="382" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Slemish" title="Slemish">Slemish</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_Antrim" title="County Antrim">County Antrim</a>, traditionally associated with Saint Patrick's time as a shepherd slave.</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Slieve_Patrick,_August_2009.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/93/Slieve_Patrick%2C_August_2009.JPG/220px-Slieve_Patrick%2C_August_2009.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/93/Slieve_Patrick%2C_August_2009.JPG/330px-Slieve_Patrick%2C_August_2009.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/93/Slieve_Patrick%2C_August_2009.JPG/440px-Slieve_Patrick%2C_August_2009.JPG 2x" data-file-width="4272" data-file-height="2848" /></a><figcaption>Saint Patrick's statue at Saul, County Down</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Croagh_Patrick_-_geograph.org.uk_-_186660.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Croagh_Patrick_-_geograph.org.uk_-_186660.jpg/220px-Croagh_Patrick_-_geograph.org.uk_-_186660.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Croagh_Patrick_-_geograph.org.uk_-_186660.jpg/330px-Croagh_Patrick_-_geograph.org.uk_-_186660.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Croagh_Patrick_-_geograph.org.uk_-_186660.jpg/440px-Croagh_Patrick_-_geograph.org.uk_-_186660.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption>Saint Patrick's Oratory at the top of <a href="/wiki/Croagh_Patrick" title="Croagh Patrick">Croagh Patrick</a>, County Mayo</figcaption></figure>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Slemish" title="Slemish">Slemish</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_Antrim" title="County Antrim">County Antrim</a> and <a href="/wiki/Killala_Bay" title="Killala Bay">Killala Bay</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_Mayo" title="County Mayo">County Mayo</a></li></ul>
<dl><dd>When captured by raiders, there are two theories as to where Patrick was enslaved. One theory is that he herded sheep in the countryside around Slemish. Another theory is that Patrick herded sheep near Killala Bay, at a place called Fochill.</dd></dl>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Glastonbury_Abbey" title="Glastonbury Abbey">Glastonbury Abbey</a>, <a href="/wiki/Somerset" title="Somerset">Somerset</a>, <a href="/wiki/UK" class="mw-redirect" title="UK">UK</a></li></ul>
<dl><dd>It is claimed that he was buried within the Abbey grounds next to the high altar, which has led to many believing this is why Glastonbury was popular among Irish pilgrims. It is also believed that he was 'the founder and the first Abbot of Glastonbury Abbey.' <sup id="cite_ref-auto_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> This was recorded by <a href="/wiki/William_of_Malmesbury" title="William of Malmesbury">William of Malmesbury</a> in his document "De antiquitate Glastoniensis ecclesiae (Concerning the Antiquity of Glastonbury)" that was compiled between 1129 and 1135, where it was noted that "After converting the Irish and establishing them solidly in the Catholic faith he returned to his native land, and was led by guidance from on high to Glastonbury. There he came upon certain holy men living the life of hermits. Finding themselves all of one mind with Patrick they decided to form a community, and elected him as their superior. Later, two of their members resided on the Tor to serve its Chapel."<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> Within the grounds of the Abbey lies <a href="/wiki/St._Patrick%27s_Chapel,_Glastonbury" title="St. Patrick&#39;s Chapel, Glastonbury">St. Patrick's Chapel, Glastonbury</a> which is a site of pilgrimage to this day. The well known Irish Scholar <a href="/wiki/James_Carney_(scholar)" title="James Carney (scholar)">James Carney</a> also elaborated on this claim and wrote "it is possible that Patrick, tired and ill at the end of his arduous mission felt released from his vow not to leave Ireland, and returned to the monastery from which he had come, which might have been Glastonbury".<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> It is also another  possible burial site of the saint, where it is documented he has been "interred in the Old Wattle Church".<sup id="cite_ref-auto_135-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup></dd></dl>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Saul_Monastery" title="Saul Monastery">Saul Monastery</a> (from <a href="/wiki/Irish_language" title="Irish language">Irish</a>: <i lang="ga">Sabhall Phádraig</i>, meaning 'Patrick's barn')<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<dl><dd>It is claimed that Patrick founded his first church in a barn at Saul, which was donated to him by a local chieftain called <a href="/wiki/Saint_Dichu" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Dichu">Dichu</a>. It is also claimed that Patrick died at Saul or was brought there between his death and burial. Nearby, on the crest of Slieve Patrick, is a huge statue of Patrick with bronze panels showing scenes from his life.</dd></dl>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Hill_of_Slane" class="mw-redirect" title="Hill of Slane">Hill of Slane</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_Meath" title="County Meath">County Meath</a></li></ul>
<dl><dd><a href="/wiki/Muirchu_moccu_Machtheni" class="mw-redirect" title="Muirchu moccu Machtheni">Muirchu moccu Machtheni</a>, in his highly mythologised seventh-century <i>Life of Patrick</i>, says that Patrick lit a <a href="/wiki/Paschal_Full_Moon" class="mw-redirect" title="Paschal Full Moon">Paschal</a> fire on this hilltop in 433 in defiance of <a href="/wiki/High_King_of_Ireland" title="High King of Ireland">High King</a> <a href="/wiki/L%C3%B3egaire_mac_N%C3%A9ill" title="Lóegaire mac Néill">Laoire</a>. The story says that the fire could not be doused by anyone but Patrick, and it was here that he explained the Holy Trinity using the shamrock.</dd></dl>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Croagh_Patrick" title="Croagh Patrick">Croagh Patrick</a>, County Mayo (from <a href="/wiki/Irish_language" title="Irish language">Irish</a>: <i lang="ga">Cruach Phádraig</i>, meaning 'Patrick's stack')<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<dl><dd>It is claimed that Patrick climbed this mountain and fasted on its summit for the forty days of <a href="/wiki/Lent" title="Lent">Lent</a>. Croagh Patrick draws thousands of pilgrims who make the trek to the top on the last Sunday in July.</dd></dl>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Lough_Derg_(Ulster)" class="mw-redirect" title="Lough Derg (Ulster)">Lough Derg</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_Donegal" title="County Donegal">County Donegal</a> (from <a href="/wiki/Irish_language" title="Irish language">Irish</a>: <i lang="ga">Loch Dearg</i>, meaning 'red lake')<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<dl><dd>It is claimed that Patrick killed a large serpent on this lake and that its blood turned the water red (hence the name). Each August, pilgrims spend three days fasting and praying there on Station Island.</dd></dl>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Armagh" title="Armagh">Armagh</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_Armagh" title="County Armagh">County Armagh</a></li></ul>
<dl><dd>It is claimed that Patrick founded a church here and proclaimed it to be the most holy church in Ireland. Armagh is today the primary seat of both the Catholic Church in Ireland and the Church of Ireland, and both cathedrals in the town are named after Patrick.</dd></dl>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Downpatrick" title="Downpatrick">Downpatrick</a>, County Down (from <a href="/wiki/Irish_language" title="Irish language">Irish</a>: <i lang="ga">Dún Pádraig</i>, meaning 'Patrick's stronghold')<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability"><span title="The material near this tag failed verification of its source citation(s). (August 2018)">failed verification</span></a></i>&#93;</sup></li></ul>
<dl><dd>It is claimed that Patrick was brought here after his death and buried in the grounds of Down Cathedral.</dd></dl>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:St._Patrick%27s_Well.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/St._Patrick%27s_Well.jpg/220px-St._Patrick%27s_Well.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="300" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/St._Patrick%27s_Well.jpg/330px-St._Patrick%27s_Well.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/St._Patrick%27s_Well.jpg/440px-St._Patrick%27s_Well.jpg 2x" data-file-width="528" data-file-height="720" /></a><figcaption>Stone found below St. Patrick's Well. St. Patrick's Cathedral, Dublin, Ireland.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Other places named after Saint Patrick include:
</p>
<ul><li>Patrickswell Lane, a well in <a href="/wiki/Drogheda" title="Drogheda">Drogheda</a> Town where St. Patrick opened a monastery and baptised the townspeople.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ardpatrick,_County_Limerick" title="Ardpatrick, County Limerick">Ardpatrick</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_Limerick" title="County Limerick">County Limerick</a> (from <a href="/wiki/Irish_language" title="Irish language">Irish</a>: <i lang="ga">Ard Pádraig</i>, meaning 'high place of Patrick')<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability"><span title="The material near this tag failed verification of its source citation(s). (August 2018)">failed verification</span></a></i>&#93;</sup></li>
<li>Patrick Water (Old Patrick Water), <a href="/wiki/Elderslie,_Scotland" title="Elderslie, Scotland">Elderslie</a>, <a href="/wiki/Renfrewshire" title="Renfrewshire">Renfrewshire</a>. from Scots' Gaelic "AlltPadraig" meaning Patrick's Burn<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Patrickswell" title="Patrickswell">Patrickswell</a> or Toberpatrick, County Limerick (from <a href="/wiki/Irish_language" title="Irish language">Irish</a>: <i lang="ga">Tobar Phádraig</i>, meaning 'Patrick's well')<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>St Patrick's Well,<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Patterdale" title="Patterdale">Patterdale</a></li>
<li>Three churches in the <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.carlislediocese.org.uk/">Diocese of Carlisle</a><sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> are dedicated to St Patrick, they are all within the historic county of <a href="/wiki/Westmorland" title="Westmorland">Westmorland</a>: St Patrick's <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.patterdaletoday.co.uk/church/church-history/">Patterdale</a>, at the head of <a href="/wiki/Ullswater" title="Ullswater">Ullswater</a> (the present church was built in the 19th Century but the chapel in Patricksdale is mentioned in a charter of 1348<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup>); St Patrick's <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.carlislediocese.org.uk/acny/church/15/">Bampton</a>, near <a href="/wiki/Shap" title="Shap">Shap</a>; St Patrick's <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.carlislediocese.org.uk/acny/church/262/">Preston Patrick</a> near <a href="/wiki/Kirkby_Lonsdale" title="Kirkby Lonsdale">Kirkby Lonsdale</a>.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/St_Patrick%27s_Chapel,_Heysham" title="St Patrick&#39;s Chapel, Heysham">St Patrick's Chapel, Heysham</a>, a ruined chapel near <a href="/wiki/St_Peter%27s_Church,_Heysham" title="St Peter&#39;s Church, Heysham">St Peter's Church, Heysham</a>, Lancashire. The chapel dates from the 8th Century.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/St_Patrick%27s_Island" title="St Patrick&#39;s Island">St Patrick's Island</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_Dublin" title="County Dublin">County Dublin</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Old_Kilpatrick" title="Old Kilpatrick">Old Kilpatrick</a>, near Dumbarton, Scotland from "Cill Phàdraig," Patrick's Church, a claimant to his birthplace</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/St_Patrick%27s_Isle" title="St Patrick&#39;s Isle">St Patrick's Isle</a>, off the <a href="/wiki/Isle_of_Man" title="Isle of Man">Isle of Man</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/St._Patricks,_Newfoundland_and_Labrador" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Patricks, Newfoundland and Labrador">St. Patricks, Newfoundland and Labrador</a>, a community in the Baie Verte district of Newfoundland</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Llanbadrig" title="Llanbadrig">Llanbadrig</a> (church), Ynys Badrig (island), Porth Padrig (cove), Llyn Padrig (lake), and Rhosbadrig (heath) on the island of <a href="/wiki/Anglesey" title="Anglesey">Anglesey</a> in Wales</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Templepatrick" title="Templepatrick">Templepatrick</a>, County Antrim (from <a href="/wiki/Irish_language" title="Irish language">Irish</a>: <i lang="ga">Teampall Phádraig</i>, meaning 'Patrick's church')<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>St Patrick's Hill, Liverpool, on old maps of the town near to the former location of "St Patrick's Cross"<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Parroquia San Patricio y Espiritu Santo. <a href="/wiki/Loiza" class="mw-redirect" title="Loiza">Loiza</a>, <a href="/wiki/Puerto_Rico" title="Puerto Rico">Puerto Rico</a>. The site was initially mentioned in 1645 as a chapel. The actual building was completed by 1729, is one of the oldest churches in the Americas and today represents the faith of many Irish immigrants that settled in Loiza by the end of the 18th century. Today it is a museum.</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_literature">In literature</span></h2>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Pedro_Calder%C3%B3n_de_la_Barca" title="Pedro Calderón de la Barca">Pedro Calderón de la Barca</a> wrote <i>El Purgatorio de San Patricio</i> in 1634.<sup id="cite_ref-PLT_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PLT-153">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Robert_Southey" title="Robert Southey">Robert Southey</a> wrote a <a href="/wiki/Ballad" title="Ballad">ballad</a> called "Saint Patrick's Purgatory", first published in 1798, based on popular legends surrounding the saint's name.<sup id="cite_ref-PLT_153-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PLT-153">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Patrick is mentioned in a 17th-century ballad about "<a href="/wiki/St._George_and_the_Dragon_(ballad)" title="St. George and the Dragon (ballad)">Saint George and the Dragon</a>"</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Stephen_R._Lawhead" title="Stephen R. Lawhead">Stephen R. Lawhead</a> wrote the fictional <i>Patrick: Son of Ireland</i> loosely based on the saint's life, including imagined accounts of training as a druid and service in the Roman army before his conversion.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The 1999 historical novel <i>Let Me Die in Ireland</i> by <a href="/wiki/Anabaptist" class="mw-redirect" title="Anabaptist">Anabaptist</a> author and attorney <a href="/wiki/David_Bercot" title="David Bercot">David Bercot</a> is based on the documented facts of Patrick's life rather than the legend, and suggests implications of his example for Christians today.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_film">In film</span></h2>
<ul><li><i><a href="/wiki/St._Patrick:_The_Irish_Legend" title="St. Patrick: The Irish Legend">St. Patrick: The Irish Legend</a></i> is a 2000 television historical drama film about the saint's life. Patrick is portrayed by <a href="/wiki/Patrick_Bergin" title="Patrick Bergin">Patrick Bergin</a>.</li>
<li><i>The Patron Saint of Ireland</i> is a 2020 film based on Patrick's own writings and the earliest traditions. Patrick is portrayed by Seán Ó Meallaigh, with Robert McCormack playing him when he is younger and <a href="/wiki/John_Rhys-Davies" title="John Rhys-Davies">John Rhys-Davies</a> in later life. It is available on <a href="/wiki/Netflix" title="Netflix">Netflix</a> in UK and Ireland.</li></ul>

<li id="cite_note-3"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-3">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite class="citation web cs1"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.history.com/topics/st-patricks-day/who-was-saint-patrick">"Who Was St. Patrick?"</a>. <i><a href="/wiki/History.com" class="mw-redirect" title="History.com">History.com</a></i>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.jtitle=History.com&amp;rft.atitle=Who+Was+St.+Patrick%3F&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.history.com%2Ftopics%2Fst-patricks-day%2Fwho-was-saint-patrick&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ASaint+Patrick" class="Z3988"></span><span class="cs1-maint citation-comment"><code class="cs1-code">{{<a href="/wiki/Template:Cite_web" title="Template:Cite web">cite web</a>}}</code>:  CS1 maint: url-status (<a href="/wiki/Category:CS1_maint:_url-status" title="Category:CS1 maint: url-status">link</a>)</span></span>

<li id="cite_note-RitschelMichallon2022-4"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-RitschelMichallon2022_4-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFRitschelMichallon2022" class="citation web cs1">Ritschel, Chelsea; Michallon, Clémence (17 March 2022). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.independent.co.uk/life-style/st-patricks-day-ireland-history-b2037858.html">"What is the meaning behind St Patrick's Day?"</a>. <i><a href="/wiki/The_Independent" title="The Independent">The Independent</a></i><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">17 March</span> 2022</span>. <q>The day of celebration, which marks the day of St Patrick's death, is a religious holiday meant to celebrate the arrival of Christianity in Ireland, and made official by the Catholic Church in the early 17th century. Observed by the Catholic Church, the Anglican Communion, the Eastern Orthodox Church, and the Lutheran Church, the day was typically observed with services, feasts and alcohol.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.jtitle=The+Independent&amp;rft.atitle=What+is+the+meaning+behind+St+Patrick%27s+Day%3F&amp;rft.date=2022-03-17&amp;rft.aulast=Ritschel&amp;rft.aufirst=Chelsea&amp;rft.au=Michallon%2C+Cl%C3%A9mence&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.independent.co.uk%2Flife-style%2Fst-patricks-day-ireland-history-b2037858.html&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ASaint+Patrick" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>

<li id="cite_note-28"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-28">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="Public Domain" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/6/62/PD-icon.svg/12px-PD-icon.svg.png" decoding="async" width="12" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/6/62/PD-icon.svg/18px-PD-icon.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/6/62/PD-icon.svg/24px-PD-icon.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="196" data-file-height="196" /></span></span>&#160;<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFHerbermann2011" class="citation encyclopaedia cs1">Herbermann, Charles, ed. (2011). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.newadvent.org/cathen/11554a.htm">"St. Patrick"</a>. <i><a href="/wiki/Catholic_Encyclopedia" title="Catholic Encyclopedia">Catholic Encyclopedia</a></i>. New York: Robert Appleton Company.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=bookitem&amp;rft.atitle=St.+Patrick&amp;rft.btitle=Catholic+Encyclopedia&amp;rft.place=New+York&amp;rft.pub=Robert+Appleton+Company&amp;rft.date=2011&amp;rft_id=http%3A%2F%2Fwww.newadvent.org%2Fcathen%2F11554a.htm&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ASaint+Patrick" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-29"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-29">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFMacNeill1926" class="citation journal cs1">MacNeill, Eoin (1926). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://archive.org/details/papersirishacad00macnuoft">"The Native Place of St. Patrick"</a>. <i>Proceedings of the Royal Irish Academy</i>. Dublin: Hodges, Figgis: 118–40<span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">17 March</span> 2013</span>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=article&amp;rft.jtitle=Proceedings+of+the+Royal+Irish+Academy&amp;rft.atitle=The+Native+Place+of+St.+Patrick&amp;rft.pages=118-40&amp;rft.date=1926&amp;rft.aulast=MacNeill&amp;rft.aufirst=Eoin&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Farchive.org%2Fdetails%2Fpapersirishacad00macnuoft&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ASaint+Patrick" class="Z3988"></span> noting that the western coasts of southern Scotland and northern England held little to interest a raider seeking quick access to booty and numerous slaves, while the southern coast of Wales offered both. In addition, the region was home to <a href="/wiki/U%C3%AD_Liath%C3%A1in" title="Uí Liatháin">Uí Liatháin</a> and possibly also y<a href="/wiki/D%C3%A9isi" title="Déisi">Déisi</a> settlers during this time, so Irish raiders would have had the contacts to tell them precisely where to go to quickly obtain booty and capture slaves. MacNeill also suggests a possible home town in Wales based on naming similarities, but allows that the transcription errors in manuscripts make this little more than an educated guess.</span>
</li>

<li id="cite_note-95"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-95">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">There may well have been Christian "Irish" people in Britain at this time; <a href="/wiki/Goidelic_languages" title="Goidelic languages">Goidelic-speaking</a> people were found on both sides of the <a href="/wiki/Irish_Sea" title="Irish Sea">Irish Sea</a>, with Irish being spoken from <a href="/wiki/Cornwall" title="Cornwall">Cornwall</a> to <a href="/wiki/Argyll" title="Argyll">Argyll</a>. The influence of the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Dyfed" title="Kingdom of Dyfed">Kingdom of Dyfed</a> may have been of particular importance. See <a href="#CITEREFCharles-Edwards2000">Charles-Edwards 2000</a>, pp.&#160;161–72; <a href="#CITEREFDark2000">Dark 2000</a>, pp.&#160;188–90; <a href="#CITEREFÓ_Cróinín1995">Ó Cróinín 1995</a>, pp.&#160;17–18; <a href="#CITEREFThomas1981">Thomas 1981</a>, pp.&#160;297–300</span>
</li>

</li>

<li id="cite_note-132"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-132">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The bell was formerly known as "The Bell of <span class="nowrap">St Patrick's</span> Will" (<i><span title="Irish-language text"><i lang="ga">Clog an eadhachta Phatraic</i></span></i>),<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> in reference to a medieval forgery which purported to have been the saint's <a href="/wiki/Last_will_and_testament" class="mw-redirect" title="Last will and testament">last will and testament</a>.</span>
